State evolution beyond first-order methods I: Rigorous predictions and finite-sample guarantees


Abstract
† † Authors listed in alphabetical order. MC contributed to this work while he was a Miller fellow at UC Berkeley. We develop a toolbox for exact analysis of iterative algorithms on a class of high-dimensional nonconvex optimization problems with random data. While prior work has shown that low-dimensional statistics of (generalized) first-order methods can be predicted by a deterministic recursion known as state evolution, our focus is on developing such a prediction for a more general class of algorithms. We provide a state evolution for any method whose iterations are given by (possibly interleaved) first-order and saddle point updates, showing two main results. First, we establish a rigorous state evolution prediction that holds even when the updates are not coordinate-wise separable. Second, we establish finite-sample guarantees bounding the deviation of the empirical updates from the established state evolution. In the process, we develop a technical toolkit that may prove useful in related problems. One component of this toolkit is a general Hilbert space lifting technique to prove existence and uniqueness of a convenient parameterization of the state evolution. Another component of the toolkit combines a generic application of Bolthausen’s conditioning method with a sequential variant of Gordon’s Gaussian comparison inequality, and provides additional ingredients that enable a general finite-sample analysis.

1
Introduction

Iterative algorithms underpin several methods in statistics and engineering. For instance, they are employed in decoders for wireless communication
(
Tse and Viswanath 2005
;
Richardson and Urbanke 2008
)
, as general purpose methods in convex and nonconvex optimization
(
Boyd and Vandenberghe 2004
;
Nocedal and Wright 2006
;
Jain and Kar 2017
)
, in the training of large-scale machine learning models
(
Bottou et al. 2018
;
Lan 2020
)
, and as powerful methods in the study of average-case computational complexity
(
Gamarnik et al. 2022
)
. From an analysis point of view, geometric properties inherent to the problem can be used to obtain rough quantitative bounds on the error dynamics of iterative algorithms even on nonconvex landscapes
(
Chi et al. 2019
;
Ge et al. 2016
;
Ge et al. 2017
, e.g.)
. However, these bounds may in some cases be (i) overly conservative when the data is random, as is often the case in statistical learning and signal processing problems
(
Blumensath and Davies 2008
;
Agarwal et al. 2012
)
, or (ii) unavailable in the absence of global problem structure
(
Nemirovski and Yudin 1983
;
Nesterov 2018
)
. Motivated by the need for sharp average-case analysis of optimization algorithms, we study the
precise
dynamics of iterative methods with random data, with the aim of producing deterministic recursions that accurately track low-dimensional functionals of the iterates. In the literature, it is common to refer to predictions of this flavor using the umbrella terminology of
state evolution

(
Donoho et al. 2009
)

To set the stage, let
n,d\in\mathbb{N}
denote a sample size and parameter dimension, respectively, and let
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
have entries
(X_{ij})_{1\leq i\leq n,1\leq j\leq d}\overset{\mathsf{iid}}{\sim}\mathsf{N}(0,1/d)
. Iterative algorithms with random data can often be viewed as methods that make use of the matrix
\bm{X}
to produce sequences
\{(\bm{u}_{k},\bm{v}_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
A canonical update that produces such a sequence is a
general first-order method
(GFOM), which we refer to throughout as just a first-order update. This update is specified by (potentially nonseparable) Lipschitz functions
f_{k+1}^{u}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
f_{k+1}^{v}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
and given by

\bm{u}_{k+1}=f_{k+1}^{u}\bigl(\bm{X}\bm{v}_{k}\bigr)\quad\text{ and }\quad\bm{v}_{k+1}=f_{k+1}^{v}\bigl(-\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{u}_{k}\bigr),

where these functions can have additional dependence on previous iterates. See Section
2
for the precise definition of this general class of updates and examples. First-order updates encompass not just gradient-based methods on canonical loss functions, but can also capture other methods that are not necessarily based on minimizing a loss
(
Berthier et al. 2020
;
Rangan 2011
;
Lou et al. 2025
, e.g.)
.

The development of state evolution predictions for first-order updates dates back to approximate message passing (AMP) (see
Donoho et al. 2009
;
Bayati and Montanari 2011
and
Feng et al. 2022
for background). The reduction of general first-order methods to AMP was first given by
Celentano et al. 2020
.
Recent years have seen a flurry of work leveraging state evolution for first-order methods in a variety of different settings including gradient flows
(
Celentano et al. 2021
;
Bordelon and Pehlevan 2023
)
, stochastic gradient methods
(
Gerbelot et al. 2024
;
Mignacco et al. 2020
;
Mannelli et al. 2019
)
, inference along the trajectory
(
Bellec and Tan 2024
;
Han and Xu 2024
)
, and in sampling applications
(
Fan et al. 2025
;
Liang et al. 2023
)
, to name a few. While the aforementioned papers are based on either AMP state evolution, Gaussian conditioning arguments, and/or dynamical mean field theory, trajectory predictions for various first-order methods have also been derived via deterministic equivalents rooted in random matrix theory
(see, e.g.,
Collins-Woodfin et al. 2024
;
Ferbach et al. 2025
, and the references therein)
or via somewhat more direct arguments
(
Tan and Vershynin 2023
;
Arous et al. 2021
;
Arous et al. 2024
;
Abbe et al. 2023
)
.

While many practical methods can be written as first-order updates, higher-order methods that are commonplace in numerical optimization do not execute this type of update.
Consider for instance iterative methods for
M
-estimation problems in which we would like to minimize the objective
\ell(\bm{X}\bm{v})
over the decision variable
\bm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
\ell:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a smooth, strongly convex function. In poorly conditioned problems, a numerically stable and preferred algorithm is the proximal point method
(
Drusvyatskiy 2018
;
Rockafellar 1976
, see, e.g.,)
, which forms the iterates
\{\bm{v}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
by solving a regularized optimization problem

\bm{v}_{k+1}=\argmin_{\bm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\;\Bigl\{\ell(\bm{X}\bm{v})+\frac{1}{2\alpha_{k}}\|\bm{v}-\bm{v}_{k}\|_{2}^{2}\Bigr\}
at each iteration.
Evidently, such an iteration cannot be written as a first-order update. However, since the convex conjugate of a smooth, strongly convex function is also smooth and strongly convex, we may write each iteration as the min-max problem

(\bm{u}_{k+1},\bm{v}_{k+1})=\underset{\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\bm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathsf{saddle}}\;\Bigl\{\bm{u}^{\top}\bm{X}\bm{v}-\ell^{\star}(\bm{u})+\frac{1}{2\alpha_{k}}\|\bm{v}-\bm{v}_{k}\|_{2}^{2}\Bigr\},

where
\ell^{\star}
denotes the Fenchel–Legendre conjugate of
\ell
.
More generally, many complex methods — for potentially nonconvex optimization problems — can be written as
saddle point updates
of the above form. In general, such an update can be written terms of two (potentially nonseparable) strongly convex and smooth penalty functions
\phi_{k+1}^{u}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
\phi_{k+1}^{v}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
as

(\bm{u}_{k+1},\bm{v}_{k+1})=\underset{\bm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{p},\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}{\mathsf{saddle}}\Big\{\bm{u}^{\top}\bm{X}\bm{v}-\phi_{k+1}^{u}(\bm{u})+\phi_{k+1}^{v}(\bm{v})\Big\},

where these functions can have additional dependence on previous iterates.
In Section
2
, we provide a precise definition of this general class of updates as well as additional examples.

In contrast to their first-order counterparts, higher-order and saddle updates have received relatively sparse attention from a state evolution perspective, aside from now-classical papers studying the one-shot saddle update for
M
-estimation problems
(
Bean et al. 2013
;
Donoho and Montanari 2016
;
Thrampoulidis et al. 2018
)
. For iterative updates, a few papers have considered the simplified sample-split setting, in which independent, fresh data is used at every iteration. Deterministic trajectory predictions have recently been analyzed to obtain concrete convergence guarantees of algorithms such as alternating minimization
(
Chandrasekher et al. 2023
;
Chandrasekher et al. 2024
)
, stochastic prox-linear methods
(
Lou et al. 2024
)
, and iteratively reweighted least squares
(
Kaushik et al. 2024
)
. In an effort to go beyond sample-split algorithms, non-rigorous replica predictions for the dynamics of a similar higher-order method were provided in
Okajima and Takahashi 2025
. A complementary body of recent work leverages random matrix theory to upper bound the dynamics of a higher-order optimization method termed “Hessian descent”
(
Montanari and Subag 2025
)
; see also papers by
(
Subag 2021
;
Jekel et al. 2025
)
. Two-step variants of saddle updates were also considered by
Celentano and Montanari 2024
and
Celentano and Wainwright 2023
in the context of analyzing debiased statistical estimators.
Complex methods have also been considered in two-stage optimization problems in which either (i) a spectral method is used for initialization followed by a first-order method such as AMP to locally refine the estimates
(
Mondelli and Venkataramanan 2021
)
or (ii) a first-order method is applied to localize the iterates and probe landscape properties of complicated random optimization problems
(
Celentano et al. 2023
;
Celentano 2024
)
.

Contributions and organization

The main contribution of this paper is to develop a rigorous state evolution for iterative methods whose updates can be written as either a first-order update or a saddle point update, as well as interleaved variants of such updates. In Section
2
, we formalize the setting and provide several more examples. We then present our two main results in Section
3
. Our first main result, Theorem
3.1
, provides an explicit description of the state evolution, accompanied by a proof of uniqueness. Our second main result, Theorem
3.2
provides finite-sample guarantees for the adherence of the empirical iterations to their state evolution predictions. The proofs of the two theorems are then provided in Sections
4
and
5
, respectively. We conclude with a discussion in Section
6
, and some ancillary and technical proofs are postponed to the appendices. In a companion paper, we provide an infinite-dimensional, canonical representation of state evolution in our setting, as well as concrete convergence consequences for particular iterative algorithms.

Let us highlight some technical takeaways from the present paper. To our knowledge, our parameterization of the state evolution is nonstandard but transparently allows — in Theorem
3.1
— for a proof of existence and uniqueness in nonseparable settings. We believe that similar parameterizations and proof techniques can be applied to other related problems. In addition, our techniques for proving Theorem
3.2
involve several pieces that may be of potential independent interest. To handle successive saddle updates, we build on Bolthausen’s conditioning argument
(
Bolthausen 2014
)
and introduce a sequential variant of the convex Gaussian minmax theorem (CGMT) that may may find broader applications; the one-shot variant
(
Gordon 1985
;
Gordon 1988
;
Stojnic 2013
;
Thrampoulidis et al. 2015
)
has been extensively used in the literature. Finally, our technique for establishing finite-sample concentration results relies on stability properties of the established state evolution, and is different from existing techniques based on directly handling AMP iterations. Details are discussed in Section
3
.

Notation

Throughout, we will let
\Lambda=n/d
for convenience.
For a positive integer
m
, we use
[m]
to denote the set of natural numbers less than or equal to
m
. We let
\mathbbm{1}\{\cdot\}
denote the
indicator function. Denote by
\mathbb{B}_{2}(r;\bm{\theta})
the
\ell_{2}
ball of radius
r
centered at
\bm{\theta}
. Let
\mathbb{S}^{k}_{\geq 0}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
denote the set of positive, semi-definite symmetric matrices. For two sequences of non-negative reals
\{f_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
and
\{g_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
, we use
f_{n}\lesssim g_{n}
to indicate that
there is a universal positive constant
C
such that
f_{n}\leq Cg_{n}
for all

n\geq 1
. Throughout, we use
(c,C,\ldots)
to denote universal
positive constants, and their values may change from line to line.

We denote by
\mathsf{N}(\bm{\mu},\bm{\Sigma})
a normal distribution with mean
\bm{\mu}
and covariance matrix
\bm{\Sigma}
. For a covariance matrix
\bm{K}\in\mathbb{S}^{k}_{\geq 0}
, a collection of random vectors
\{\bm{z}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
is distributed as
\bm{z}_{1},\bm{z}_{2},\ldots,\bm{z}_{k}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\bm{K}\otimes\bm{I}_{m})
means that
\{\bm{z}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
form a collection of centered Gaussian random vectors with pairwise covariance matrices
\mathbb{E}[\bm{z}_{\ell}\bm{z}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{\top}]=K_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}\bm{I}_{m}
. Equivalently, the matrix
\bm{Z}_{k}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times k}
having
i
-th column
\bm{z}_{i}
has i.i.d. rows each drawn from the Gaussian distribution
\mathsf{N}(0,\bm{K})
. We say that
X\overset{(d)}{=}Y
for two random variables
X
and
Y
that are equal in distribution.

Given a measurable space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F})
and a measure
P
, we let
L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},P)
denote the Hilbert space of square-integrable functions on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F})
with respect to the measure
P
. When the context is clear, we abuse notation and simply write
L^{2}
. When
\Omega
itself is an inner product space, for two elements
X,X^{\prime}\in L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},P)
, we write
\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle_{L^{2}}=\mathbb{E}\langle X,X^{\prime}\rangle
. For a topological space
\mathbb{X}
, we will let
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{X})
denote its Borel
\sigma
-algebra. We will use square brackets
[\cdot]
to enclose the arguments of a linear functional
\mathsf{f}:L^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and the usual round brackets
(\cdot)
to enclose the argument of a function
f:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.

For a pair of matrices
\bm{A},\bm{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times k}
, we define the operation
\langle\!\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\!\rangle:\mathbb{R}^{m\times k}\times\mathbb{R}^{m\times k}
as
\langle\!\langle\bm{A},\bm{B}\rangle\!\rangle:=\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
. We will additionally write
\langle\!\langle\bm{A},\bm{B}\rangle\!\rangle_{L^{2}}=\mathbb{E}\langle\!\langle\bm{A},\bm{B}\rangle\!\rangle
with expectations taken entry-wise. For a matrix
\bm{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, we will let
\|\bm{A}\|_{\infty}
denote its maximum absolute entry and
\kappa(\bm{A}):=\sigma_{\max}(\bm{A})/\sigma_{\min}(\bm{A})
denote its condition number.

For
\mu\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and a convex function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, we define the proximal operator
\mathrm{prox}_{\mu f}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
as

\mathrm{prox}_{\mu f}(x):=\argmin_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Bigl\{f(y)+\frac{1}{2\mu}\|y-x\|_{2}^{2}\Bigr\}.

We say that a multivariate function
h:\mathbb{R}^{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{p}
is
L
-Lipschitz if
\|h(x)-h(x^{\prime})\|_{2}\leq L\|x-x^{\prime}\|_{2}
for all
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
.
Let
(\mathbb{X},\|\cdot\|)
be a normed space and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. We say that a function
\psi:\mathbb{X}^{\otimes k}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is order-
2
pseudo-Lipschitz with constant
L
if, for all
(x_{1},\ldots x_{k}),(x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{k})\in\mathbb{X}^{\otimes k}
,

\lvert\psi(x)-\psi(x^{\prime})\rvert\leq L\cdot\biggl(1+\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\|x_{\ell}\|+\|x^{\prime}_{\ell}\|\biggr)\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\|x_{\ell}-x^{\prime}_{\ell}\|\quad\text{ and }\quad\lvert\psi(0,\ldots,0)\rvert\leq L.

We adopt the convention that for the function
h:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
defined as
h(\bm{x})=\|\bm{x}\|_{2}
, we have
\partial h(0)=0
.

2
Formal setting

In this section, we formally introduce the type of iterative algorithms considered in this paper, and provide several examples of such algorithms.

2.1
Family of iterative algorithms

As alluded to in Section
1
, we consider iterations that make use of the matrix
\bm{X}
with (a possible mix of) first-order and saddle updates, accommodating algorithms whose iterates may involve some form of auxiliary randomness. Let
\{\bm{\varepsilon}_{u,k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\overset{\mathsf{iid}}{\sim}\mathsf{N}(0,\bm{I}_{n}/n)
and
\{\bm{\varepsilon}_{v,k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\overset{\mathsf{iid}}{\sim}\mathsf{N}(0,\bm{I}_{d}/d)
denote auxiliary randomness independent of
\bm{X}
. We consider iterative algorithms which form sequences of pairs
\{(\bm{u}_{k},\bm{v}_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which are initialized as

\bm{u}_{1}=f_{1}^{u}(\bm{\varepsilon}_{u,1}),\quad\text{ and }\quad\bm{v}_{1}=f_{1}^{v}(\bm{\varepsilon}_{v,1}).

(1)

Here
f_{1}^{u}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and
f_{1}^{v}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
are, respectively,
L
-Lipschitz functions of their arguments. Note that this allows for both random initialization as well as deterministic initializations — in the latter case we can choose
(f_{1}^{u},f_{1}^{v})
to be suitable constant functions so that
\bm{u}_{1}=f_{1}^{u}(0)
and
\bm{v}_{1}=f_{1}^{v}(0)
.

After
k\in\mathbb{N}
iterations, we collect the previous iterates
\bm{u}_{1},\ldots,\bm{u}_{k}
and
\bm{v}_{1},\ldots,\bm{v}_{k}
into matrices

\bm{U}_{k}=[\bm{u}_{1}\;|\;\bm{u}_{2}\;|\;\cdots\;|\;\bm{u}_{k}]\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\bm{V}_{k}=[\bm{v}_{1}\;|\;\bm{v}_{2}\;|\;\cdots\;|\;\bm{v}_{k}]\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k},

and collect the auxiliary randomness
\{\bm{\varepsilon}_{u,\ell}\}_{1\leq\ell\leq k}
and
\{\bm{\varepsilon}_{v,\ell}\}_{1\leq\ell\leq k}
into matrices
\bm{E}_{u,k}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
and
\bm{E}_{v,k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}
similarly.

At time
k+1
, we then take one of two possible updates: a first-order or saddle-point update, defined below.

Definition 2.1

(First-order update)
.

Let
f_{k+1}^{u}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
f_{k+1}^{v}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
be
L
-Lipschitz functions. A (generalized) first-order update at iteration
k+1
is given by

\displaystyle\bm{u}_{k+1}

\displaystyle=f_{k+1}^{u}(\bm{X}\bm{v}_{k};\bm{U}_{k},\bm{E}_{u,k}),\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\bm{v}_{k+1}=f_{k+1}^{v}(-\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{u}_{k};\bm{V}_{k},\bm{E}_{v,k}).

(2a)

Definition 2.2

(Saddle update)
.

Let
\phi^{u}_{k+1}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
\phi_{k+1}^{v}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
denote a pair of
\mu
-strongly convex and
L
-smooth functions. A saddle update is given by

\displaystyle(\bm{u}_{k+1},\bm{v}_{k+1})

\displaystyle=\underset{\bm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{p},\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}{\mathsf{saddle}}\Big\{\bm{u}^{\top}\bm{X}\bm{v}-\phi_{k+1}^{u}(\bm{u};\bm{U}_{k},\bm{E}_{u,k})+\phi_{k+1}^{v}(\bm{v};\bm{V}_{k},\bm{E}_{v,k})\Big\}.

(2b)

For brevity below, we often drop the dependence on the auxiliary randomness
(\bm{E}_{u,k},\bm{E}_{v,k})
in the definitions of the functions
(f^{u}_{k+1},f^{v}_{k+1},\phi^{u}_{k+1},\phi^{v}_{k+1})
. Equipped with these definitions, let us now give several examples of iterative methods that fall under the umbrella of these updates.

2.2
Illustrative examples

To illustrate, we write two first-order methods in the form (
2a
) and two saddle methods in the form (
2b
); note that another example is given by the proximal point update from Section
1
. We note that the general form of iterations can also cover updates that interleave saddle and first-order updates
(
Mondelli and Venkataramanan 2021
;
Celentano et al. 2023
, see, e.g.)
.

Example 1: Gradient methods:

Our first example is gradient descent for
M
-estimation in generalized linear models. Given a smooth (i.e.
C^{1}
) function
\ell
, we are interested in optimizing a loss
\mathcal{R}(\bm{\theta})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(\langle\bm{x}_{i},\,\bm{\theta}\rangle;y_{i})
via gradient descent iterates

\displaystyle\bm{\theta}_{t+1}=\bm{\theta}_{t}-\frac{\eta}{n}\bm{X}^{\top}\ell^{\prime}(\bm{X}\bm{\theta}_{t};\bm{y}).

(3)

Here we write
\ell^{\prime}(\bm{X}\bm{\theta}_{t};\bm{y})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
to denote a vector whose
i
-th entry is given by
\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{x}_{i},\,\bm{\theta}\rangle;y_{i})
and the derivative is taken with respect to the first argument.

To write Eq. (
3
) as a first-order update according to Definition
2.1
, we define
(\bm{u}_{\ell},\bm{v}_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 0}
as follows

\displaystyle\bm{u}_{\ell}=\ell^{\prime}(\bm{X}\bm{v}_{\ell};\bm{y})\qquad

and

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{\ell}=\bm{v}_{\ell-1}\quad

\displaystyle\text{ if $\ell$ odd},

\displaystyle\bm{u}_{\ell}=\bm{u}_{\ell-1}\qquad

and

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{\ell}=\bm{v}_{\ell-1}-\eta\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{u}_{\ell-1}\quad

\displaystyle\text{ if $\ell$ even}.

We then take
\bm{\theta}_{k}=\bm{v}_{2(k-1)}
with initialization
\bm{v}_{0}=\bm{\theta}_{1}
and
\bm{u}_{0}=0
.
\clubsuit

Example 2: Approximate message passing (AMP):

The approximate message passing algorithm in standard form
(
Bayati and Montanari 2011
;
Javanmard and Montanari 2013
;
Berthier et al. 2020
)
for iterates
\bm{p}_{k}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\bm{q}_{k}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and nonlinearities
\tau_{k}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
e_{k}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is given by the iteration
1
1

1

Note that we have an additional factor
\Lambda
appearing in the Onsager terms since the typical AMP iteration is stated using a random matrix having entries of variance
1/n
, whereas our random matrix
\bm{X}
has entries of variance
1/d
.

\begin{gathered}\bm{p}_{k+1}=\bm{X}^{\top}\tau_{k}(\bm{q}_{k})-\mathsf{a}_{k}e_{k}(\bm{p}_{k}),\qquad\bm{q}_{k}=\bm{X}e_{k}(\bm{p}_{k})-\mathsf{b}_{k-1}\tau_{k-1}(\bm{q}_{k-1}),\end{gathered}

(4)

where
the scalars
\mathsf{a}_{k}=\frac{1}{d}\mathsf{div}[\tau_{k}](\bm{q}_{k})
and
\mathsf{b}_{k}=\frac{1}{d}\mathsf{div}[e_{k}](\bm{p}_{k})
give rise to Onsager correction terms. Here
\mathsf{div}[f]
is the divergence of
f
, given by the sum of its coordinate-wise derivatives.

To write Eq. (
4
) as a first-order update according to Definition
2.1
, we introduce
(\bm{u}_{\ell},\bm{v}_{\ell})_{\ell\geq-1}
via

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{4k}=e_{k}(\bm{v}_{4k-1})\qquad

and

\displaystyle\bm{u}_{4k}=\bm{u}_{4k-1},

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{4k+1}=\bm{v}_{4k}\qquad

and

\displaystyle\bm{u}_{4k+1}=\bm{X}\bm{v}_{4k}-\mathsf{b}_{k-1}\tau_{k-1}(\bm{u}_{4k}),

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{4k+2}=\bm{v}_{4k+1}\qquad

and

\displaystyle\bm{u}_{4k+2}=\tau_{k}(\bm{u}_{4k+1}),

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{4k+3}=\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{u}_{4k+2}-\mathsf{a}_{k}e_{k}(\bm{v}_{4k+2})\qquad

and

\displaystyle\bm{u}_{4k+3}=\bm{u}_{4k+2}.

We then take
\bm{p}_{k+1}=\bm{v}_{4k+3}
and
\bm{q}_{k}=\bm{u}_{4k+1}
with initialization
\bm{v}_{-1}=\bm{p}_{1}
and
\bm{u}_{-1}=0
.
\clubsuit

Example 3: Tikhonov regularized nonlinear least squares for single-index models:

Let
\mathcal{Q}
denote a distribution over latent variables and suppose we have a known link function
g:\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{Q}\to\real{R}
. Consider a set of
n
i.i.d. covariate-response pairs
\{(\bm{x}_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
, generated according to the (random) single-index model
y_{i}=g(\bm{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}_{*};q_{i})+\sigma\epsilon_{i}
, with
(\bm{x}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\overset{\mathsf{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathsf{N}(0,\bm{I}_{d}/d)
,
(q_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\overset{\mathsf{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathcal{Q}
,
\epsilon_{i}\overset{\mathsf{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathsf{N}(0,1)
and with a prior that
\bm{\theta}_{*}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\bm{\Sigma})
for some positive definite matrix
\bm{\Sigma}
.

A canonical family of algorithms to optimize the log-posterior in these problems
(
Chandrasekher et al. 2023
)
is a weighted least squares update, in which we choose a Tikhonov regularization proportional to
\|\bm{\theta}\|^{2}_{\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}}=\bm{\theta}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}\bm{\theta}
and some weight function
w:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
and then execute the iterations

\displaystyle\bm{\theta}_{k+1}=\argmin_{\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\;\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Bigl(w(\bm{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}_{k},y_{i})y_{i}-\bm{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}\Bigr)^{2}+\lambda\|\bm{\theta}\|_{\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}}^{2}=\argmin_{\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\;\frac{1}{2}\bigl\|w(\bm{X}\bm{\theta}_{k},\bm{y})\odot\bm{y}-\bm{X}\bm{\theta}\bigr\|_{2}^{2}+\lambda\|\bm{\theta}\|_{\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}}^{2}

(5)

for some
\lambda>0
,
where we stack the weights
w(\bm{x}_{i}^{\top}\theta,y)
to form the vector
w(\bm{X}\bm{\theta}_{k},\bm{y})
and
\odot
denotes the Hadamard product.
Examples of such updates are alternating minimization for phase retrieval
(
Gerchberg and Saxton 1972
;
Fienup 1982
)
— in which we take
w(\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{\theta},y)=\mathsf{sgn}(\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{\theta})
— and expectation maximization (EM) for mixtures of linear regressions
(
Dempster et al. 1977
;
Balakrishnan et al. 2017
)
— in which we take

w(\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{\theta},y)=\frac{\phi((y-\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{\theta})/\sigma)}{\phi((y-\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{\theta})/\sigma)+\phi((y+\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{\theta})/\sigma)},

where
\phi
denotes the standard Gaussian density.

The iterations (
5
) can equivalently be written as

(\bm{u}_{k+1},\bm{\theta}_{k+1})=\underset{\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}{\mathsf{saddle}}\;\biggl\{\bm{u}^{\top}\bm{X}\bm{\theta}-\bm{u}^{\top}\bm{y}\odot w(\bm{X}\bm{\theta}_{k},\bm{y})-\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}\|_{2}^{2}+\lambda\|\bm{\theta}\|_{\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}}^{2}\biggr\},

which is an instance of the saddle-point updates (
2b
) with
\bm{v}_{k}=\bm{\theta}_{k}
and functions
\phi_{k+1}(\bm{\theta})=\lambda\|\bm{v}\|_{2}^{2}
and
\phi^{u}_{k+1}(\bm{u})=\|\bm{u}\|_{2}^{2}/2+\bm{u}^{\top}\bm{y}\odot w(\bm{X}\bm{v}_{k},\bm{y})
. Furthermore, notice that the KKT conditions of the saddle problems for each
2\leq\ell\leq k
yield that
\bm{X}\bm{v}_{\ell}=\bm{y}\odot w(\bm{X}\bm{\theta}_{\ell-1},\bm{y})+\bm{u}_{k}
. Thus
\phi^{u}_{k+1}(\bm{u})
depends on the past iterates only through the matrix
\bm{U}_{k}
.
\clubsuit

Example 4: Prox-linear for nonlinear least squares problems:

Suppose that given
n
i.i.d. covariate response pairs
\{(\bm{x}_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
, we are interested in solving the nonlinear least squares problem

\min_{\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\;\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl\{y_{i}-g(\bm{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta})\bigr\}^{2},

where
g:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is assumed (for simplicity) to be strictly increasing and differentiable. Such loss functions are of interest in, for instance, nonlinear computed tomography
(see, e.g.,
Fridovich-Keil et al. 2024
, and the references therein)
. A canonical iterative method to perform this minimization is the
prox-linear method
(
Lewis and Wright 2016
;
Drusvyatskiy et al. 2021
)
, whose iterates
\{\bm{\theta}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
can be written according to the updates

\bm{\theta}_{k+1}=\argmin_{\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl\{y_{i}-g(\bm{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}_{k})-g^{\prime}(\bm{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}_{k})\bm{x}_{i}^{\top}(\bm{\theta}-\bm{\theta}_{k})\bigr\}^{2}+\frac{1}{2\alpha_{k}}\|\bm{\theta}-\bm{\theta}_{k}\|_{2}^{2}.

Note that taking the stepsize
\alpha_{k}\rightarrow\infty
, this reduces to the familiar Gauss–Newton method
(see, e.g.,
Nocedal and Wright 2006
, Chapter 10)
.
Write the residuals as
r_{i}:=y_{i}-g(\bm{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}_{k})
and collect these into the vector
\bm{r}_{k}=[r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}]^{\top}
. Also define the diagonal matrix
\bm{D}_{k}=\text{\rm diag}(g^{\prime}(\bm{x}_{1}^{\top}\bm{\theta}_{k}),\ldots,g^{\prime}(\bm{x}_{n}^{\top}\bm{\theta}_{k}))
and make the change of variables
\bm{v}=\bm{\theta}-\bm{\theta}_{k}
. Then we can more concisely write each iteration as

\bm{v}_{k+1}=\argmin_{\bm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\;\bigl\|\bm{r}_{k}-\bm{D}_{k}\bm{X}\bm{v}\bigr\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{2\alpha_{k}}\|\bm{v}\|_{2}^{2}=\argmin_{\bm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sup_{\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\;\bm{z}^{\top}\bm{D}_{k}\bm{X}\bm{v}-\bm{z}^{\top}\bm{r}_{k}-\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{z}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{2\alpha_{k}}\|\bm{v}_{k}\|_{2}^{2},

where in the second relation we used the fact that the Fenchel–Legendre conjugate of
\bm{x}\mapsto\|\bm{x}\|_{2}^{2}/2
is itself. Making the substitution
\bm{u}=\bm{D}_{k}\bm{z}
we thus have

(\bm{u}_{k+1},\bm{v}_{k+1})=\underset{\bm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}{\mathsf{saddle}}\;\biggl\{\bm{u}^{\top}\bm{X}\bm{v}-\bm{u}^{\top}\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}\bm{r}_{k}-\frac{1}{2}\bigl\|\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}\bm{u}\bigr\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{2\alpha_{k}}\|\bm{v}_{k}\|_{2}^{2}\biggr\},

where we note that since
g
is strictly increasing, the inverse
\bm{D}_{k}^{-1}
always exists.
\clubsuit

3
Main results

Having given several examples, we now state our main results for the general iterations that we set up in Section
2
. First, in Theorem
3.1
, we provide a state evolution recursion that covers these methods, and show rigorously the existence and uniqueness of this state evolution. Second, in Theorem
3.2
, we establish finite-sample guarantees showing that low-dimensional functions of the empirical iterates (
1
)-(
2
) are predicted by deterministic functionals of the state evolution.

3.1
State evolution

Recall that
\Lambda=n/d
. The state evolution specifies two joint distributions on different probability spaces, indexed by the iteration
k\in\mathbb{N}
. The first is a joint distribution over random matrices
\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},\widehat{\bm{U}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},\bm{H}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
while the second is a joint distribution over random matrices
\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k},\widehat{\bm{V}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k},\bm{G}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}
. When
k=1
, these satisfy

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1}=\bm{v}_{1},\qquad\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1}=\bm{u}_{1},\qquad\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1}=0,\qquad\widehat{\bm{v}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1}=0,\\
\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1}\sim\mathsf{N}({\bm{0}},\|\bm{u}_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\cdot{\mathbf{I}}_{d}/d)\qquad\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1}\sim\mathsf{N}({\bm{0}},\|\bm{v}_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\cdot{\mathbf{I}}_{n}/n).\end{gathered}

For further indices
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k>1
, they satisfy

\begin{gathered}\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1},\ldots,\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\sim\mathsf{N}({\bm{0}},\bm{K}^{g}_{k}\otimes{\mathbf{I}}_{d}/d),\quad\text{ and }\quad\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1},\ldots,\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\sim\mathsf{N}({\bm{0}},\bm{K}^{h}_{k}\otimes{\mathbf{I}}_{n}/n),\\
\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\in\mathsf{span}\bigl(\{\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell}\}_{1\leq\ell\leq k}\bigr),\quad\text{ and }\quad\widehat{\bm{v}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\in\mathsf{span}\bigl(\{\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell}\}_{1\leq\ell\leq k}\bigr).\end{gathered}

(8)

For first order updates at iteration
k
, they additionally satisfy

\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}=f_{k}^{u}\bigl(\sqrt{\Lambda}\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k-1}-\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k-1};\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k-1}\bigr),\quad\text{ and }\quad\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}=f_{k}^{v}\bigl(\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k-1}-\widehat{\bm{v}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k-1};\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k-1}\bigr),

(9)

where we recall that
f_{k}^{u},f_{k}^{v}
were the Lipschitz update functions specified in Definition
2.1
. On the other hand, for saddle updates at iteration
k
, they satisfy

\sqrt{\Lambda}\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}-\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}=\nabla\phi_{k}^{u}(\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k};\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k-1}),\quad\text{ and }\quad\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}-\widehat{\bm{v}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}=\nabla\phi_{k}^{v}(\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k};\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k-1}).

(10)

These random variables additionally satisfy the expectation relations

\displaystyle\bm{K}^{g}_{k}=\langle\!\langle\bm{G}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{G}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\rangle\!\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\langle\!\langle\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\rangle\!\rangle_{L^{2}},\qquad

\displaystyle\bm{K}^{h}_{T}=\langle\!\langle\bm{H}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{H}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\rangle\!\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\langle\!\langle\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\rangle\!\rangle_{L^{2}},

(11)

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\bm{G}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\rangle\!\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\langle\!\langle\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\widehat{\bm{U}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\rangle\!\rangle_{L^{2}},\qquad

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\sqrt{\Lambda}\bm{H}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\rangle\!\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\langle\!\langle\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\widehat{\bm{V}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\rangle\!\rangle_{L^{2}}.

Note that above, we abuse notation and write
L^{2}
to mean one of two quantities: either

L^{2}\Bigl(\underbrace{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\cdots\mathbb{R}^{d}}_{k\text{ times}},\mathcal{B}\bigl(\underbrace{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\cdots\mathbb{R}^{d}}_{k\text{ times}}\bigr),\mathsf{N}(0,\bm{I}_{d})^{\otimes k}\Bigr)\quad\text{ or }\quad L^{2}\Bigl(\underbrace{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\cdots\mathbb{R}^{n}}_{k\text{ times}},\mathcal{B}\bigl(\underbrace{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\cdots\mathbb{R}^{n}}_{k\text{ times}}\bigr),\mathsf{N}(0,\bm{I}_{n})^{\otimes k}\Bigr).

Note that since the functions
\{f_{k}^{u},f_{k}^{v}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{\nabla\phi^{u}_{k},\nabla\phi^{v}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
are Lipschitz, all of the random vectors in the previous displays are elements of the appropriate
L^{2}
spaces described above.

Our first main result proves that the equations defining the state evolution have a unique solution.

Theorem 3.1
.

For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a unique pair of joint distributions
\mathsf{Law}(\bm{G}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\widehat{\bm{V}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k})
and
\mathsf{Law}(\bm{H}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\widehat{\bm{U}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k})
that satisfy Eqs. (
3.1
), (
8
), (
9
), (
10
), and (
11
).

We provide the proof of Theorem
3.1
in Section
4
.

Our parameterization (
3.1
)–(
11
) of the state evolution may seem nonstandard, but as revealed in the proof of Theorem
3.1
, it is directly useful in establishing existence and uniqueness guarantees. To illustrate the parameterization, we show that it recovers two existing state evolutions in the literature.

Our first example consists of linear estimation with AMP
Bayati and Montanari 2011
in a pure noise problem, in which the dynamics are given by

\bm{p}_{k+1}=\lambda_{k}\cdot\bigl(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{q}_{k}+\Lambda\bm{p}_{k}\bigr),\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\bm{q}_{k}=\bm{y}-\bm{X}\bm{p}_{k}+\lambda_{k-1}\bm{q}_{k-1},

where
\lambda_{k}
is a user-defined parameter and
\bm{y}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{n})
. For this simple iteration, the reduction to our first-order methods in Definition
2.1
is simpler than that in Example 2. Indeed, we take

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{2k}=\bm{v}_{2k-1}\qquad

and

\displaystyle\bm{u}_{2k}=\bm{y}-\bm{X}\bm{v}_{2k-1}+\lambda_{k-1}\bm{u}_{2k-1},

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{2k+1}=\lambda_{k}\cdot\bigl(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{u}_{2k}+\Lambda\bm{v}_{2k}\bigr)\qquad

and

\displaystyle\bm{u}_{2k+1}=\bm{u}_{2k}.

(12)

In Appendix
A.1
, we show that the state evolution in Eqs. (
3.1
)–(
11
) recovers
Bayati and Montanari 2011
with
v
in that equation set to
0
. In particular, we show that
\|\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{2k}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\tau_{k}^{2}
where
\tau_{k}
satisfies the recursion

\tau_{k}^{2}=\sigma^{2}+\Lambda\lambda_{k-1}^{2}\tau_{k-1}^{2}.

For our second example, we consider the asymptotic description of the behavior of
M
-estimators as in
(
Thrampoulidis et al. 2018
;
Bellec and Koriyama 2023
)
, where one is interested in understanding asymptotic properties of

\bm{v}^{\mathrm{M}}=\argmin_{\bm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\;L(\bm{v}):=\frac{1}{d}\rho\bigl(\bm{y}-\bm{X}\bm{v}\bigr)+\frac{\lambda}{d}f(\bm{v}),

where
\rho
is a data-fitting term and
f
is understood as a penalty. To simplify the discussion, we suppose that
\bm{y}=0
. In Appendix
A.2
, we show that the asymptotic properties of this problem are characterized by the fixed point equations

\displaystyle\alpha^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl\{\mathsf{prox}_{\lambda\nu^{-1}f}\bigl(\nu^{-1}\beta Z\bigr)\bigr\}^{2}\Bigr],

(13a)

\displaystyle\Lambda^{-1}\beta^{2}\kappa^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl\{\alpha W-\mathsf{prox}_{\kappa\rho}(\alpha W)\bigr\}^{2}\Bigr],

(13b)

\displaystyle\delta^{-1}\nu\alpha\kappa

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[W\cdot\bigl\{\alpha W-\mathsf{prox}_{\kappa\rho}(\alpha W)\bigr\}\Bigr],\quad\text{and }

(13c)

\displaystyle\kappa\beta

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[Z\cdot\mathsf{prox}_{\lambda\nu^{-1}f}(\nu^{-1}\beta Z)\Bigr],,

(13d)

where
Z,W\sim\mathsf{N}(0,1)
are independent of all other randomness. These equations coincide with the set of fixed point equations in
Bellec and Koriyama 2023
. Note that taking one saddle step in our state evolution and applying Theorem
3.1
immediately implies the existence and uniqueness of a solution to the fixed point equations in (
13
), hence recovering (under stronger assumptions) the guarantee of
Bellec and Koriyama 2023
.

Let us remark briefly on our technique for proving Theorem
3.1
. Our proof is inspired by the variational approach of
Montanari et al. 2025
, which obtains existence and uniqueness of a solution to a system of nonlinear equations obtained post-scalarization of the auxiliary optimization generated by the convex Gaussian minmax theorem (CGMT). In particular, they construct an appropriate infinite-dimensional optimization problem whose KKT conditions coincide with the fixed point equations. Our proof follows this general recipe inductively, but without explicitly scalarizing the auxiliary objective; note that this is important in our setting since the functions defining our saddle update are not necessarily separable. At each iteration, when the update is a saddle update, we construct an appropriate variational problem and show that its KKT conditions coincide with the state evolution. On the other hand, if the update is first-order, we argue the existence and uniqueness of the state evolution from first principles.

3.2
Finite-sample guarantees

We now show that the state evolution (
3.1
)–(
11
) is operationally useful, in that it can make predictions about the iterates
(\bm{u}_{k},\bm{v}_{k})_{\geq 1}
.
We begin with some notation. Since
\widehat{\bm{v}}_{k}^{\mathrm{SE}}\in\mathsf{span}(\{\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k^{\prime}}\}_{k^{\prime}\leq k})
and
\widehat{\bm{u}}_{k}^{\mathrm{SE}}\in\mathsf{span}(\{\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k^{\prime}}\}_{k^{\prime}\leq k})
, there must exist matrices
\bm{L}_{k}^{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
and
\bm{L}_{k}^{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
such that

\widehat{\bm{v}}_{k}^{\mathrm{SE}}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\bigl(L^{v}_{k}\bigr)_{k,\ell}\bm{v}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{SE}}\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{\bm{u}}_{k}^{\mathrm{SE}}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\bigl(L^{u}_{k}\bigr)_{k,\ell}\bm{u}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{SE}}.

Using these coefficients, we further define, for
\ell\in[k]
, the vectors

\displaystyle\bm{g}_{\ell}:=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\bigl(L^{v}_{\ell}\bigr)_{j,\ell}\bm{v}_{j}-\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{u}_{\ell}\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{h}_{\ell}:=\Lambda^{-1/2}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\bigl(L^{u}_{\ell}\bigr)_{\ell,j}\bm{u}_{j}+\bm{X}\bm{v}_{\ell}\Big),

(14)

and collect these into the matrices
\bm{G}_{k}=[\bm{g}_{1}\;|\;\bm{g}_{2}\;|\;\cdots\;|\;\bm{g}_{k}]\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}
and
\bm{H}_{k}=[\bm{h}_{1}\;|\;\bm{h}_{2}\;|\;\cdots\;|\;\bm{h}_{k}]\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
.
Before stating the theorem, we require the following regularity assumption.

Assumption 1
.

There exist universal constants
(\mu,L,K)
such that the following is true. For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the functions
\phi_{k}^{v},\phi_{k}^{u}
are
\mu
-strongly convex and
L
-smooth, and the functions
f^{u}_{k},f^{v}_{k}
are
L
-Lipschitz. Moreover, we have
\|\nabla\phi_{k}^{u}(0)\|_{2}\vee\|\nabla\phi_{k}^{v}(0)\|_{2}\vee\|f^{u}_{k}(0)\|_{2}\vee\|f^{v}_{k}(0)\|_{2}\leq K
.

Assumption 2
.

The matrices
\bm{K}^{g}_{k},\bm{K}^{h}_{k}
in Eq. (
8
) are invertible for each
k
.

We note that Assumption
2
is similar to the non-degeneracy assumption in
Berthier et al. 2020
and can be removed using similar techniques therein or by using approximate Cholesky decompositions as in
Celentano and Wainwright 2023
. We elect to state our theorem under this simplifying assumption as it allows to more easily expose the ideas underlying the proof.

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
T\in\mathbb{N}
, suppose that Assumptions
1
and
2
hold, and that, for each,
k\in[T]
, the pair
(\bm{u}_{k},\bm{v}_{k})
is obtained using either a first order update as in Definition
2.1
or a saddle update as in Definition
2.2
. Further, let
\bm{U}_{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times T}
and
\bm{V}_{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times T}
be defined as

\bm{U}_{T}=[\bm{u}_{1}\;|\;\bm{u}_{2}\;|\;\cdots\;|\;\bm{u}_{T}]\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\bm{V}_{T}=[\bm{v}_{1}\;|\;\bm{v}_{2}\;|\;\cdots\;|\;\bm{v}_{T}].

Let
\psi_{d}:\mathbb{R}^{d\times T}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times T}
and
\psi_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{n\times T}\times\mathbb{R}^{n\times T}
be order-
2
pseudo-Lipschitz functions. There exists a pair of constants
c_{\mathrm{SE}},C_{\mathrm{SE}}>0
, depending only on the state evolution and the tuple
(\mu,L,K)
such that if
\delta\in(e^{-c_{\mathrm{SE}}n},1)
, then with probability at least
1-\delta
, we have

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\psi_{d}(\bm{V}_{T},\bm{G}_{T})-\mathbb{E}\bigl[\psi_{d}(\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{T},\bm{G}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{T})\bigr]\Bigr\rvert\;\vee\;\Bigl\lvert\psi_{n}(\bm{U}_{T},\bm{H}_{T})-\mathbb{E}\bigl[\psi_{n}(\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{T},\bm{H}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{T})\bigr]\Bigr\rvert\leq C_{\mathrm{SE}}\bigl(T!\bigr)^{2}\Bigl(\frac{\log(10T^{2}/\delta)}{n}\Bigr)^{2^{-T}}.

(15)

We provide the proof of this theorem in Section
5
. The key takeaway is that empirical quantities (e.g.
\psi_{d}(\bm{V}_{T},\bm{G}_{T})
) are accurately predicted by deterministic quantities (e.g.
\mathbb{E}\bigl[\psi_{d}(\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{T},\bm{G}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{T})\bigr]
) that are computable as simple expectations of Gaussian functionals. In particular, these are themselves low-dimensional integrals provided the test and iteration functions are “simple” (e.g. separable). In that sense, the state evolution provides predictions of the behavior of the random iterates, and Theorem
3.2
additionally gives non-asymptotic fluctuation guarantees showing consistency in
n
. A few further remarks are in order.

First, a careful inspection of the proof reveals that the RHS of the bound (
15
) can be improved to

C_{\mathrm{SE}}\bigl(T!\bigr)^{2}\Bigl(\frac{\log(10T^{2}/\delta)}{n}\Bigr)^{2^{-(\tau\vee 1)}}
, where
\tau
is the number of saddle updates. In particular,
if every update is first order, we obtain the sharper bound

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\psi_{d}(\bm{V}_{T},\bm{G}_{T})-\mathbb{E}\bigl[\psi_{d}(\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{T},\bm{G}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{T})\bigr]\Bigr\rvert\;\vee\;\Bigl\lvert\psi_{n}(\bm{U}_{T},\bm{H}_{T})-\mathbb{E}\bigl[\psi_{n}(\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{T},\bm{H}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{T})\bigr]\Bigr\rvert\leq C_{\mathrm{SE}}\bigl(T!\bigr)^{2}\Bigl(\frac{\log(10T^{2}/\delta)}{n}\Bigr)^{1/2},

(16)

Since, by Stirling’s inequality,
T!\leq e^{2}(T/e)^{T+1}
, this implies that (ignoring logarithmic factors) we have a deviation bound scaling as
C_{\mathrm{SE}}T^{2T+2}n^{-1/2}
. If the constant
C_{\mathrm{SE}}
is a universal constant (e.g. independent of
T,n,d
), then this bound implies that the state evolution is valid for
T=o(\log{n}/\log\log{n})
many iterations. Results of a similar flavor were shown by
Rush and Venkataramanan 2018
for AMP (see also
Cademartori and Rush 2024
), and GFOMs (see
Han 2024
, who also provides universality guarantees). Our proof techniques, however, are distinct from these papers and arguably easier to generalize to other situations.

We remark that, in general, the appearance of
(T!)^{2}
in the bound (
16
) should not be thought of as very loose. Indeed, the quantity
C_{\mathrm{SE}}
itself can depend on
T
through properties of the state evolution. Thus, at the level of generality of the first-order update (
2a
), one should not expect a tighter deviation bound than one scaling as
C^{T}n^{-1/2}
and hence the state evolution can only be accurate for
o(\log n)
iterations. To see this, set some constant
C>1
and consider the explosive iteration

\displaystyle\bm{u}_{k+1}=\sqrt{\frac{C}{\Lambda}}\bm{X}\bm{v}_{k}\quad\text{ and }\quad\bm{v}_{k+1}=\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{u}_{k},

which is a first-order update of the form (
2a
).
With any fixed, unit-norm initializations
(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{v}_{1})
, it can be verified that
\|\bm{u}_{T+1}\|_{2}^{2}=C^{T}\mathsf{trace}\Big((\frac{\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}}{\Lambda})^{T}\Big)
. It can also be verified that
\mathbb{E}[\|\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{T+1}\|_{2}^{2}]=\int(Cx)^{T}\nu(dx)
, where
\nu
is the Marchenko–Pastur measure. By standard results in random matrix theory
(
Bai and Silverstein 2010
;
Couillet and Liao 2022
)
, we should expect the deviation to scale as

\big|\|\bm{u}_{T+1}\|_{2}^{2}-\mathbb{E}[\|\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{T+1}\|_{2}^{2}]\big|=\left|C^{T}\mathsf{trace}\Big((\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X})/\Lambda)^{T}\Big)-\int(Cx)^{T}\nu(dx)\right|\asymp C^{T}\Big(\frac{1}{n}\Big)^{1/2}

with constant probability.

While the argument above shows the optimality of the
n^{-1/2}
rate for first-order methods in their most general form, sharper concentration results may be obtainable by exploiting specific properties of the state evolution in particular methods (see, e.g.,
Li and Wei 2022
;
Li and Wei 2024
). We also note that for saddle updates, the exponent
2^{-T}
in Eq. (
15
) is an artifact of our analysis. This arises because of a sequential application of Gordon’s Gaussian comparison inequality, and each application typically yields deviation bounds of a suboptimal order
(see, e.g.,
Chandrasekher et al. 2023
;
Miolane and Montanari 2021
, for further discussion)
.

As previously noted, our proof technique for establishing Theorem
3.2
may be of independent interest. We introduce a sequential variant of the convex Gaussian minmax theorem (CGMT) to accommodate successive saddle updates, and apply it in conjunction with Bolthausen’s conditioning technique
(
Bolthausen 2014
)
. To establish finite-sample concentration results, we leverage certain stability properties of the established state evolution in conjunction with a concise proof of sub-exponential concentration for pseudo-Lipschitz functions (see Lemma
C.5
).

We now proceed to proofs of the two main theorems.

4
Proof of Theorem
3.1
: State evolution has unique solution

Since the state evolution at time
k+1
depends on its definition at time
k
, our proof will proceed via induction. In Section
4.1
, we present the base case
k=1
. The induction hypothesis is set up formally in Section
4.2
. The bulk of the proof is in the induction step, which is presented in Section
4.3
for a first-order update and Section
4.4
for a saddle update.

4.1
Base case:
k=1

The pair
(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{v}_{1})
is fixed independently of the randomness in the data
\bm{X}
, and the tuple
(\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1},\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1},\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1},\widehat{\bm{v}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1})
is chosen—in particular, uniquely—based on this pair. In particular, since both
\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1}
and
\widehat{\bm{v}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1}
are equal to zero, we have the trivial inclusions
\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1}\in\mathsf{span}\{\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1}\}
and
\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1}\in\mathsf{span}\{\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1}\}
. Furthermore, the random vectors
\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1}
and
\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1}
are drawn from standard Gaussians scaled by
\|\bm{u}_{1}\|_{2}/\sqrt{d}
and
\|\bm{v}_{1}\|_{2}/\sqrt{n}
, respectively, so that we have the scalar relations
\bm{K}^{g}_{1}=\|\bm{u}_{1}\|^{2}_{2}
and
\bm{K}^{h}_{1}=\|\bm{v}_{1}\|^{2}_{2}
. It remains to verify the fixed point equations (
11
), but this is also immediate since

\displaystyle\langle\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1},\,\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1}\rangle_{L^{2}}=\|\bm{u}_{1}\|_{2}^{2}=\langle\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1},\,\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1}\rangle_{L^{2}},\qquad

\displaystyle\langle\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1},\,\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1}\rangle_{L^{2}}=\|\bm{v}_{1}\|_{2}^{2}=\langle\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1},\,\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1}\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\langle\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1},\,\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1}\rangle_{L^{2}}=\langle\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1},\,\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1}\rangle_{L^{2}}=0,\qquad

\displaystyle\langle\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1},\,\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1}\rangle_{L^{2}}=\langle\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1},\,\widehat{\bm{v}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1}\rangle_{L^{2}}=0,

which completes the base case. We turn now to stating the induction hypothesis.

4.2
Induction hypothesis

Suppose that the fixed-point equations up to iteration
k
are uniquely defined. In other words, we have that for covariance matrices
\bm{K}^{g}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
and
\bm{K}^{h}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
and Gaussian random matrices
\bm{G}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\bm{K}^{g}_{k}\otimes\bm{I}_{d}/d)
and
\bm{H}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\bm{K}^{h}_{k}\otimes\bm{I}_{n}/n)
, the system of equations

\displaystyle\bm{K}^{g}_{k}=\langle\!\langle\bm{G}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{G}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\rangle\!\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\langle\!\langle\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\rangle\!\rangle_{L^{2}},\qquad

\displaystyle\bm{K}^{h}_{k}=\langle\!\langle\bm{H}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{H}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\rangle\!\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}\langle\!\langle\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\rangle\!\rangle_{L^{2}},

(17)

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\bm{G}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\rangle\!\rangle_{L^{2}},

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{=}\langle\!\langle\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\widehat{\bm{U}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\rangle\!\rangle_{L^{2}},\qquad

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\sqrt{\Lambda}\bm{H}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\rangle\!\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\overset{(d)}{=}\langle\!\langle\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\widehat{\bm{V}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\rangle\!\rangle_{L^{2}},

has a unique solution. Specifically, we have

\displaystyle\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1}=\bm{v}_{1},\qquad\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1}=\bm{u}_{1},\qquad\widehat{\bm{v}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1}=0,\qquad\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1}=0,

\displaystyle\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1}\sim\mathsf{N}({\bm{0}},\|\bm{u}_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\cdot{\mathbf{I}}_{d}/d)\qquad\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{1}\sim\mathsf{N}({\bm{0}},\|\bm{v}\|_{2}^{2}\cdot{\mathbf{I}}_{n}/n),

and for all
2\leq\ell\leq k
, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{v}}{}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell}\in\mathsf{span}(\{\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell^{\prime}}\}_{\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell}),\qquad

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell}\in\mathsf{span}(\{\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell^{\prime}}\}_{\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell}),

and

\displaystyle\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell}=f^{u}_{\ell}\bigl(\sqrt{\Lambda}\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell-1}-\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell-1},\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell-1}\bigr),\qquad

\displaystyle\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell}=f^{v}_{\ell}\bigl(\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell-1}-\widehat{\bm{v}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell-1},\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell-1}\bigr)

\displaystyle\text{ if first-order update at time }\ell,

\displaystyle\sqrt{\Lambda}\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell}-\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell}=\nabla\phi_{\ell}^{u}\bigl(\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell};\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell-1}\bigr),\qquad

\displaystyle\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell}-\widehat{\bm{v}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell}=\nabla\phi_{\ell}^{v}\bigl(\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell};\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell-1}\bigr)\;\;\;

\displaystyle\text{ if saddle update at time }\ell.

Furthermore, the joint laws of the matrices
(\bm{G}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\widehat{\bm{V}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k})
and
(\bm{H}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\widehat{\bm{U}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k})
are uniquely defined.

4.3
Proof of induction step for first-order updates

Our goal is to establish that the state evolution has a unique solution at time
k+1
if we take a first-order update. We first form the upper left
k\times k
blocks of the covariance matrices
\bm{K}^{g}_{k+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{(k+1)\times(k+1)}
and
\bm{K}^{h}_{k+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{(k+1)\times(k+1)}
by setting them equal to
\bm{K}^{g}_{k}
and
\bm{K}^{h}_{k}
, respectively. We must show that for some
d
-dimensional random vector
\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}
with i.i.d. entries that is jointly Gaussian with
\bm{G}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}
and
n
-dimensional random vector
\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}
with i.i.d. entries that is jointly Gaussian with
\bm{H}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}
, we can construct
d
-dimensional random vectors
\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}
and
\widehat{\bm{v}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}
and
n
-dimensional random vectors
\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}
and
\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}
satisfying

•

The following system of equations for all
\ell=k+1,\ell^{\prime}\leq k+1
or
\ell^{\prime}=k+1,\ell\leq k+1
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\langle\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell},\,\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell^{\prime}}\rangle]

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\mathbb{E}[\langle\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell},\,\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell^{\prime}}\rangle],\qquad

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\langle\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell},\,\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell^{\prime}}\rangle]

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}\mathbb{E}[\langle\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell},\,\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell^{\prime}}\rangle]

(18)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\langle\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell},\,\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell^{\prime}}\rangle]

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{=}\mathbb{E}[\langle\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell},\,\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell^{\prime}}\rangle],\qquad

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\sqrt{\Lambda}\langle\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell},\,\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell^{\prime}}\rangle]

\displaystyle\overset{(d)}{=}\mathbb{E}[\langle\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell},\,\widehat{\bm{v}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell^{\prime}}\rangle].

•

The inclusions

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{v}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}\in\mathsf{span}(\{\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell}\}_{\ell\leq k+1}),\qquad

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}\in\mathsf{span}(\{\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell}\}_{s\leq t}).

(19)

•

The relations

\displaystyle\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}=f^{u}_{k+1}\bigl(\sqrt{\Lambda}\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}-\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k};\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\bigr),\qquad

\displaystyle\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}=f^{v}_{k+1}\bigl(\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}-\widehat{\bm{v}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k};\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\bigr).

(20)

Furthermore, we would like the joint (conditional) laws of

(\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1},\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1},\widehat{\bm{v}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1})\mid\bm{G}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\widehat{\bm{V}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\quad\text{ and }\quad(\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1},\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1},\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1})\mid\bm{H}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\widehat{\bm{U}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}

to be uniquely defined.

We now construct the random vectors satisfying the above desiderata. To this end, let
\bm{G}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\bm{K}^{g}_{k}\otimes{\mathbf{I}}_{d}/d)
,
\bm{H}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\bm{K}^{h}_{k}\otimes{\mathbf{I}}_{n}/n)
and let
\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\widehat{\bm{V}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\widehat{\bm{U}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}
be defined inductively via (
9
) and (
10
). From these, we explicitly construct the tuples
(\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1},\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1},\widehat{\bm{v}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1})
and
(\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1},\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1},\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1})
in a unique fashion, thereby ensuring that the joint laws of
(\bm{G}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1},\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1},\widehat{\bm{V}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1})
and
(\bm{H}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1},\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1},\widehat{\bm{U}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1})
are unique.

Note that, given the past, Eq. (
9
) uniquely specifies

\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}=f_{k+1}^{u}\bigl(\sqrt{\Lambda}\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}-\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k};\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\bigr)\quad\text{ and }\quad\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}=f_{k+1}^{v}\bigl(\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}-\widehat{\bm{v}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k};\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\bigr),

by definition, so that Eq. (
20
) is satisfied. It follows that the matrices
\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}
and
\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}
are uniquely specified. We thus use these to (uniquely) define

\bm{K}^{g}_{k+1}=\langle\!\langle\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1},\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}\rangle\!\rangle_{L^{2}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\bm{K}^{h}_{k+1}=\langle\!\langle\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1},\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}\rangle\!\rangle_{L^{2}}.

By joint Gaussianity, we can write
\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}=\alpha_{k+1}\bm{\xi}_{g}+\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}^{\mathrm{SE}}
, where
\bm{\xi}_{g}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,{\mathbf{I}}_{d}/d)
, independently of everything else and
\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}^{\mathrm{SE}}\in\mathsf{span}(\{\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell}\}_{1\leq\ell\leq k})
so that
\langle\!\langle\bm{G}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1},\bm{G}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}\rangle\!\rangle_{L^{2}}=\bm{K}^{g}_{k+1}
as well as
\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}=\beta_{k+1}\bm{\xi}_{h}+\widetilde{\bm{h}}_{k}^{\mathrm{SE}}
, where
\bm{\xi}_{h}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,{\mathbf{I}}_{n}/n)
, independently of everything else and
\widetilde{\bm{h}}_{k}^{\mathrm{SE}}\in\mathsf{span}(\{\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell}\}_{1\leq\ell\leq k})
, so that
\langle\!\langle\bm{H}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1},\bm{H}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}\rangle\!\rangle_{L^{2}}=\bm{K}^{h}_{k+1}
. By construction, relations
(a)
and
(b)
in Eq. (
18
) are satisfied. Note further that, by the fundamental theorem of linear algebra, there exists a unique
\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}\in\mathsf{span}(\{\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell}\}_{1\leq\ell\leq k+1})
such that

\langle\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1},\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell}\rangle_{L^{2}}=\langle\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1},\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell}\rangle_{L^{2}},\quad\text{ for all }\quad\ell\in[k+1],

(21)

and similarly for
\widehat{\bm{v}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}
. This shows that the relation (
19
) holds for the uniquely defined
\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}
and
\widehat{\bm{v}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}
and that relations
(c)
and
(d)
of Eq. (
18
) hold by construction. ∎

4.4
Proof of induction step for saddle updates

The saddle update is quite a bit more involved. We begin by outlining the desiderata. Similar to the first order step, our goal is to establish that the state evolution has a unique solution at time
k+1
if we take a saddle update. We first form the upper left
k\times k
blocks of the covariance matrices
\bm{K}^{g}_{k+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{(k+1)\times(k+1)}
and
\bm{K}^{h}_{k+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{(k+1)\times(k+1)}
by setting them equal to
\bm{K}^{g}_{k}
and
\bm{K}^{h}_{k}
, respectively. We must show that for some
d
-dimensional random vector
\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}
with i.i.d. entries that is jointly Gaussian with
\bm{G}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}
and
n
-dimensional random vector
\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}
with i.i.d. entries that is jointly Gaussian with
\bm{H}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}
, we can construct
d
-dimensional random vectors
\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}
and
\widehat{\bm{v}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}
and
n
-dimensional random vectors
\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}
and
\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}
satisfying

•

The following system of equations for all
\ell=k+1,\ell^{\prime}\leq k+1
or
\ell^{\prime}=k+1,\ell\leq k+1
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\langle\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell},\,\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell^{\prime}}\rangle]

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\mathbb{E}[\langle\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell},\,\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell^{\prime}}\rangle],\qquad

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\langle\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell},\,\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell^{\prime}}\rangle]

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}\mathbb{E}[\langle\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell},\,\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell^{\prime}}\rangle]

(22)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\langle\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell},\,\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell^{\prime}}\rangle]

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{=}\mathbb{E}[\langle\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell},\,\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell^{\prime}}\rangle],\qquad

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\sqrt{\Lambda}\langle\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell},\,\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell^{\prime}}\rangle]

\displaystyle\overset{(d)}{=}\mathbb{E}[\langle\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell},\,\widehat{\bm{v}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell^{\prime}}\rangle].

•

The inclusions

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{v}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}\in\mathsf{span}(\{\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell}\}_{\ell\leq k+1}),\qquad

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}\in\mathsf{span}(\{\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell}\}_{s\leq t}).

(23)

•

The relations

\displaystyle\sqrt{\Lambda}\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}-\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}=\nabla\phi_{k+1}^{u}\bigl(\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1};\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\bigr),\qquad\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}-\widehat{\bm{v}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}=\nabla\phi_{k+1}^{v}\bigl(\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1};\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\bigr).

(24)

Furthermore, we would like the joint (conditional) laws of

(\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1},\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1},\widehat{\bm{v}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1})\mid\bm{G}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\widehat{\bm{V}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}\quad\text{ and }\quad(\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1},\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1},\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1})\mid\bm{H}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\widehat{\bm{U}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}

to be uniquely defined.

We perform the induction step by defining two Hilbert spaces as well as operators between them. We then define a useful optimization problem on these spaces that will help us argue uniqueness.

Consider auxiliary randomness
\bm{\xi}_{g}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,{\mathbf{I}}_{d}/d)
and
\bm{\xi}_{h}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,{\mathbf{I}}_{n}/n)
chosen independently of everything else.
Define the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{d}^{k+1}
as all
d
-dimensional, square integrable functions of the tuple
(\bm{G}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\widehat{\bm{V}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{g})
and the Hilbert space
2
2

2

Note that these are subspaces of the
L^{2}
spaces considered in Section
3
.

\mathcal{H}_{n}^{k+1}
of all
n
-dimensional, square integrable functions of the tuple
(\bm{H}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\widehat{\bm{U}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{h})
, i.e.
3
3

3

Recall that the standard inner product between two deterministic vectors
\bm{x},\bm{y}
of comparable dimension is denoted by
\langle\bm{x},\,\bm{y}\rangle
, and we have
\|\bm{x}\|_{2}^{2}=\langle\bm{x},\,\bm{x}\rangle
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{d}^{k+1}

\displaystyle=\{\bm{f}:\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}:\mathbb{E}[\|\bm{f}(\bm{G}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\widehat{\bm{V}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{g},)\|_{2}^{2}]<\infty\},

(25a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{n}^{k+1}

\displaystyle=\{\bm{\ell}:\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}\times\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}\times\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}:\mathbb{E}[\|\bm{\ell}(\bm{H}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\widehat{\bm{U}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{h})\|_{2}^{2}]<\infty\}.

(25b)

Equip each space with the inner product
\langle\cdot,\,\cdot\rangle_{L^{2}}
: for
\bm{f},\bm{f}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{k+1}
, we have

\langle\bm{f},\,\bm{f}^{\prime}\rangle_{L^{2}}=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\langle\bm{f}(\bm{G}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\widehat{\bm{V}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{g}),\,\bm{f}^{\prime}(\bm{G}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\widehat{\bm{V}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k},\bm{\xi}_{g})\rangle\bigr],

and with a slight abuse of notation, we also use similar conventions for
\bm{\ell},\bm{\ell}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}_{n}^{k+1}
. For
\bm{f}\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{k+1}
, we write
\|\bm{f}\|_{L^{2}}=\sqrt{\langle\bm{f},\,\bm{f}\rangle_{L^{2}}}
and similarly define
\|\bm{\ell}\|_{L^{2}}
for
\bm{\ell}\in\mathcal{H}_{n}^{k+1}
.
For a matrix
\bm{Z}=[\bm{x}_{1}\mid\cdots\mid\bm{x}_{N}]
having columns
\bm{x}_{1},\ldots,\bm{x}_{N}\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{k+1}
and another
\bm{v}\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{k+1}
, we write
\mathsf{P}_{\bm{Z}}^{\perp}\bm{v}

for the projection of
\bm{v}
onto the subspace orthogonal to the span of
\bm{x}_{1},\ldots,\bm{x}_{N}
. Orthogonal projections in the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{n}^{k+1}
are defined similarly. Also recall our convention that
\langle\!\langle\bm{Z},\bm{v}\rangle\!\rangle_{L^{2}}
is a deterministic
N
-dimensional vector with
j
-th entry equal to
\langle\bm{x}_{j},\,\bm{v}\rangle_{L^{2}}
.

For
\ell=1,2,\ldots,k
, let

\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE},\perp}_{\ell}:=\frac{\mathsf{P}_{\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell-1}}^{\perp}\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell}}{\|\mathsf{P}_{\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell-1}}^{\perp}\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell}\|_{L^{2}}},\quad\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE},\perp}_{\ell}:=\frac{\mathsf{P}_{\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell-1}}^{\perp}\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell}}{\|\mathsf{P}_{\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell-1}}^{\perp}\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell}\|_{L^{2}}},\quad\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE},\perp}_{\ell}:=\frac{\mathsf{P}_{\bm{G}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell-1}}^{\perp}\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell}}{\|\mathsf{P}_{\bm{G}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell-1}}^{\perp}\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell}\|_{L^{2}}},\;\;\text{ and }\;\;\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE},\perp}_{\ell}:=\frac{\mathsf{P}_{\bm{H}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell-1}}^{\perp}\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell}}{\|\mathsf{P}_{\bm{H}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell-1}}^{\perp}\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{\ell}\|_{L^{2}}}.

In all of these definitions, we adopt the convention that
\bm{0}/\|\bm{0}\|_{L^{2}}:=\bm{0}
. Ignoring the zero elements, note that each of the collections
\{\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE},\perp}_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{k},\{\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE},\perp}_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{k},\{\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE},\perp}_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{k},\{\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE},\perp}_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{k}
are orthonormal in their respective Hilbert spaces.

Next, define the linear operators
\mathsf{g}:\mathcal{H}_{n}^{k+1}\to\mathcal{H}_{d}^{k+1}
and
\mathsf{h}:\mathcal{H}_{d}^{k+1}\to\mathcal{H}_{n}^{k+1}
via

\mathsf{g}[\bm{u}]=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\langle\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE},\perp}_{\ell},\bm{u}\rangle_{L^{2}}\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE},\perp}_{\ell}+\|\mathsf{P}_{\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}}^{\perp}\bm{u}\|_{L^{2}}\bm{\xi}_{g},\qquad\mathsf{h}[\bm{v}]=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\langle\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE},\perp}_{\ell},\bm{v}\rangle_{L^{2}}\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE},\perp}_{\ell}+\|\mathsf{P}_{\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}}^{\perp}\bm{v}\|_{L^{2}}\bm{\xi}_{h},

(26)

and the function
\mathfrak{L}_{k+1}:\mathcal{H}_{n}^{k+1}\times\mathcal{H}_{d}^{k+1}\to\mathbb{R}
via

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}_{k+1}(\bm{u},\bm{v}):=-\langle\mathsf{g}[\bm{u}],\bm{v}\rangle_{L^{2}}+\sqrt{\Lambda}\langle\mathsf{h}[\bm{v}],\bm{u}\rangle_{L^{2}}-\mathbb{E}[\phi_{k+1}^{u}(\bm{u};\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k})]+\mathbb{E}[\phi_{k+1}^{v}(\bm{v};\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k})].

(27)

Also define the linear functions
\mathfrak{G}:\mathcal{H}_{d}^{k+1}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\mathfrak{H}:\mathcal{H}_{n}^{k+1}\to\mathbb{R}
via

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}(\bm{v}):=\langle\bm{v},\,\bm{\xi}_{g}\rangle_{L^{2}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathfrak{H}(\bm{u}):=\langle\bm{u},\,\bm{\xi}_{h}\rangle_{L^{2}},

(28)

respectively.
Finally, define the constrained saddle point problem

\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{v}\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{k+1}\\
\mathfrak{G}(\bm{v})\geq 0\end{subarray}}\;\;\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{u}\in\mathcal{H}_{n}^{k+1}\\
\mathfrak{H}(\bm{u})\geq 0\end{subarray}}\;\;\mathfrak{L}_{k+1}(\bm{u},\bm{v}).

(29)

Equipped with this setup, we next present several useful properties.

Lemma 4.1
.

The saddle problem (
29
) has a unique solution
({\widetilde{\bm{u}}},{\widetilde{\bm{v}}})\in\mathcal{H}_{n}^{k+1}\times\mathcal{H}_{p}^{k+1}
that also satisfies the equalities

\displaystyle\mathsf{g}[{\widetilde{\bm{u}}}]-\sqrt{\Lambda}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\langle\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE},\perp}_{\ell},{\widetilde{\bm{u}}}\rangle_{L^{2}}\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE},\perp}_{\ell}-\sqrt{\Lambda}\langle\bm{\xi}_{h},{\widetilde{\bm{u}}}\rangle_{L^{2}}\frac{\mathsf{P}_{\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}}^{\perp}{\widetilde{\bm{v}}}}{\|\mathsf{P}_{\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}}^{\perp}{\widetilde{\bm{v}}}\|_{L^{2}}}

\displaystyle=(\nabla\phi_{k+1}^{v})({\widetilde{\bm{v}}};\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}),

(30a)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\Lambda}\mathsf{h}[{\widetilde{\bm{v}}}]-\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\langle\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE},\perp}_{\ell},{\widetilde{\bm{v}}}\rangle_{L^{2}}\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE},\perp}_{\ell}-\langle\bm{\xi}_{g},{\widetilde{\bm{v}}}\rangle_{L^{2}}\frac{\mathsf{P}_{\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}}^{\perp}{\widetilde{\bm{u}}}}{\|\mathsf{P}_{\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}}^{\perp}{\widetilde{\bm{u}}}\|_{L^{2}}}

\displaystyle=(\nabla\phi_{t}^{u})({\widetilde{\bm{u}}};\bm{U}_{k}),

(30b)

where we use the convention that
\bm{0}/\|\bm{0}\|_{L^{2}}=\bm{0}
.

Lemma
4.1
is proved in Section
4.4.1
; operationally, it shows that provided we adopt the convention
\bm{0}/\|\bm{0}\|_{L^{2}}=\bm{0}
, the conditions (
30
)—which are effectively the KKT conditions of the
unconstrained
optimization problem
\min_{\bm{v}\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{k+1}}\;\;\max_{\bm{u}\in\mathcal{H}_{n}^{k+1}}\;\;\mathfrak{L}_{k+1}(\bm{u},\bm{v})
—are also the same conditions characterizing optimality in the
constrained
problem (
29
). This technical lemma is the key to our argument, and is crucial to establishing the following propositions.

Proposition 4.2

(Existence of fixed point solutions)
.

Let
({\widetilde{\bm{u}}},{\widetilde{\bm{v}}})\in\mathcal{H}_{n}^{k+1}\times\mathcal{H}_{d}^{k+1}
denote the solution to the saddle problem (
29
) guaranteed by Lemma
4.1
. Then the tuple
(\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1},\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1},\widehat{\bm{v}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1},\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1},\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1},\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1})
given by

\displaystyle\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}={\widetilde{\bm{u}}},\qquad\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}={\widetilde{\bm{v}}},\qquad\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}=\mathsf{g}[{\widetilde{\bm{u}}}],\qquad\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}=\mathsf{h}[{\widetilde{\bm{v}}}],

(31a)

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{u}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\langle\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE},\perp}_{\ell},{\widetilde{\bm{v}}}\rangle_{L^{2}}\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE},\perp}_{\ell}+\langle\bm{\xi}_{g},{\widetilde{\bm{v}}}\rangle_{L^{2}}\frac{\mathsf{P}_{\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}}^{\perp}{\widetilde{\bm{u}}}}{\|\mathsf{P}_{\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}}^{\perp}{\widetilde{\bm{u}}}\|_{L^{2}}},

(31b)

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{v}}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\Lambda}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\langle\bm{h}^{\mathrm{SE},\perp}_{\ell},{\widetilde{\bm{u}}}\rangle_{L^{2}}\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE},\perp}_{\ell}+\sqrt{\Lambda}\langle\bm{\xi}_{h},{\widetilde{\bm{u}}}\rangle_{L^{2}}\frac{\mathsf{P}_{\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}}^{\perp}{\widetilde{\bm{v}}}}{\|\mathsf{P}_{\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}}^{\perp}{\widetilde{\bm{v}}}\|_{L^{2}}}.

(31c)

satisfies Eqs. (
22
), (
23
) and (
24
).

Proposition
4.2
is proved in Section
4.4.2
.

Proposition 4.3

(Uniqueness of fixed point solutions)
.

If the tuple
\bigl(\bm{g}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*},\bm{v}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*},\widehat{\bm{v}}{}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*},\bm{h}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*},\bm{u}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*},\widehat{\bm{u}}{}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*}\bigr)
is a valid fixed point solution satisfying Eqs. (
22
), (
23
) and (
24
), then
(\bm{v}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*},\bm{u}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*})
is a solution to the saddle problem (
29
). Furthermore, we must have

\displaystyle\bm{g}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*}=\mathsf{g}\bigl[\bm{u}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*}\bigr],\qquad\bm{h}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*}=\mathsf{h}\bigl[\bm{v}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*}\bigr],

(32a)

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{u}}{}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\langle\bm{g}^{\mathrm{SE},\perp}_{\ell},\bm{v}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*}\rangle_{L^{2}}\bm{u}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{SE},\perp}+\langle\bm{\xi}_{g},\bm{v}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*}\rangle_{L^{2}}\frac{\mathsf{P}_{\bm{U}_{k}^{\mathrm{SE}}}^{\perp}\bm{u}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*}}{\|\mathsf{P}_{\bm{U}_{k}^{\mathrm{SE}}}^{\perp}\bm{u}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*}\|_{L^{2}}},

(32b)

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{v}}{}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\Lambda}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\langle\bm{h}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{SE},\perp},\bm{v}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*}\rangle_{L^{2}}\bm{v}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{SE},\perp}+\sqrt{\Lambda}\langle\bm{\xi}_{h},\bm{v}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*}\rangle_{L^{2}}\frac{\mathsf{P}_{\bm{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{SE}}}^{\perp}\bm{v}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*}}{\|\mathsf{P}_{\bm{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{SE}}}^{\perp}\bm{v}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*}\|_{L^{2}}}.

(32c)

Proposition
4.3
is proved in Section
4.4.3
.

Armed with these two propositions, we now complete the inductive step. On the one hand, Proposition
4.2
and Lemma
4.1
together guarantee a solution
(\bm{v}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{t},\bm{u}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{t})
to the SE fixed point equations (
22
), (
23
) and (
24
).
On the other hand, by Proposition
4.2
, any solution to the SE fixed point equations can be used to construct a solution
(\bm{v}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*},\bm{u}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*})
to the saddle problem (
29
). But since Lemma
4.1
also guarantees that the solution to the saddle problem (
29
) is unique, we must have
(\bm{v}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*},\bm{u}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*})=(\bm{v}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE}},\bm{u}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE}})
. Finally, comparing Eqs. (
32
) and Eq. (
31
) and noting that
(\bm{v}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*},\bm{u}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*})=(\bm{v}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE}},\bm{u}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE}})
, we may conclude equality of the entire tuple
(\bm{g}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*},\bm{v}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*},\widehat{\bm{v}}{}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*},\bm{h}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*},\bm{u}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*},\widehat{\bm{u}}{}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE},*})=(\bm{g}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE}},\bm{v}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE}},\widehat{\bm{v}}{}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE}},\bm{h}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE}},\bm{u}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE}},\widehat{\bm{u}}{}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{SE}})
. We have thus established existence and uniqueness of the SE fixed points at time
t
, and this completes the induction step for saddle update.
∎

4.4.1
Proof of Lemma
4.1

Since the functions
\phi_{k+1}^{u}
,
\phi_{k+1}^{v}
are strongly convex in their first arguments, the maps
\mathbb{E}[\phi^{u}_{k+1}]:\mathcal{H}_{n}^{k+1}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{E}[\phi^{v}_{k+1}]:\mathcal{H}_{d}^{k+1}\to\mathbb{R}
are also strongly convex. Furthermore, both sets
\mathcal{V}=\{\bm{v}:\mathfrak{G}(\bm{v})\geq 0\}
and
\mathcal{U}=\{\bm{u}:\mathfrak{H}(\bm{u})\geq 0\}
are convex, and on
\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{V}
the function
\mathfrak{L}_{k+1}
(
27
) is strongly concave convex.
Define the function
\overline{\mathfrak{L}}_{k+1}
mapping
\mathcal{H}_{n}^{k+1}\times\mathcal{H}_{d}^{k+1}
to values on the extended reals, via

\overline{\mathfrak{L}}_{k+1}(\bm{u},\bm{v}):=\mathfrak{L}_{k+1}(\bm{u},\bm{v})+\mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{V}}(\bm{v})-\mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{U}}(\bm{u}),

where
\mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{X}}(x)
is an indicator that takes value
0
if
x\in\mathcal{X}
and the value
+\infty
otherwise. The function
\overline{\mathfrak{L}}_{k+1}
is a proper saddle function in the terminology of
Barbu and Precupanu 2012
. Moreover, since
\mathfrak{L}_{k+1}
is strongly convex in
\bm{v}
and strongly concave in
\bm{u}
(and hence coercive in each variable respectively) on
\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{V}
, we see that
\lim_{\|\bm{u}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\bm{v}\|_{L^{2}}\rightarrow\infty,(\bm{u},\bm{v})\in\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{V}}=-\infty
, so that by
(
Barbu and Precupanu 2012
, Corollary 2.118)
, there exists a solution to the saddle point problem

\displaystyle\min_{\bm{v}\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{k+1}}\max_{\bm{u}\in\mathcal{H}_{n}^{k+1}}\overline{\mathfrak{L}}_{k+1}(\bm{u},\bm{v}).

(33)

Additionally, strong convexity of the map
\bm{v}\mapsto\max_{\bm{u}\in\mathcal{U}}\overline{\mathfrak{L}}_{k+1}(\bm{u},\bm{v})
ensures that any solution to the above saddle point problem is unique. Further,
Barbu and Precupanu 2012
implies that the subdifferential
\partial\overline{\mathfrak{L}}_{k+1}=(\partial_{\bm{v}}\overline{\mathfrak{L}}_{k+1}(\bm{u},\bm{v}),-\partial_{\bm{u}}\overline{\mathfrak{L}}_{k+1}(\bm{u},\bm{v}))
is maximally monotone and by
Barbu and Precupanu 2012
, the solution
({\widetilde{\bm{u}}},{\widetilde{\bm{v}}})
to the saddle point problem (
33
) satisfies
(\bm{0},\bm{0})\in\partial\overline{\mathfrak{L}}_{k+1}({\widetilde{\bm{u}}},{\widetilde{\bm{v}}})
. This, in turn, by dominated convergence, simplifies to the system

\displaystyle\mathsf{g}[{\widetilde{\bm{u}}}]-\sqrt{\Lambda}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\langle\bm{h}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{SE},\perp},\,{\widetilde{\bm{u}}}\rangle_{L^{2}}\bm{v}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{SE},\perp}-\sqrt{\Lambda}\langle\bm{\xi}_{h},\,{\widetilde{\bm{u}}}\rangle_{L^{2}}\cdot\partial_{\bm{v}}\left(\|\mathsf{P}_{\bm{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{SE}}}^{\perp}\bm{v}\|_{L^{2}}\right)_{\bm{v}={\widetilde{\bm{v}}}}+\lambda_{v}\bm{\xi}_{g}

\displaystyle=(\nabla\phi_{k+1}^{v})({\widetilde{\bm{v}}};\bm{V}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k})

(34a)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\Lambda}\mathsf{h}[{\widetilde{\bm{v}}}]-\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\langle\bm{g}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{SE},\perp},\,{\widetilde{\bm{v}}}\rangle_{L^{2}}\bm{u}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{SE},\perp}-\langle\bm{\xi}_{g},\,{\widetilde{\bm{v}}}\rangle_{L^{2}}\cdot\partial_{\bm{u}}\left(\|\mathsf{P}_{\bm{U}_{k}^{\mathrm{SE}}}^{\perp}\bm{u}\|_{L^{2}}\right)_{\bm{u}={\widetilde{\bm{u}}}}+\lambda_{u}\bm{\xi}_{h}

\displaystyle=(\nabla\phi_{k+1}^{u})({\widetilde{\bm{u}}};\bm{U}^{\mathrm{SE}}_{k}),

(34b)

where
\lambda_{v}\geq 0
should be interpreted as a slack variable for the constraint
\langle\bm{\xi}_{g},\,\bm{v}\rangle_{L^{2}}\geq 0
, and so
\lambda_{v}\cdot\langle\bm{\xi}_{g},\,\bm{v}\rangle_{L^{2}}=0
. Similarly,
\lambda_{u}\geq 0
should be interpreted as a slack variable for the constraint
\langle\bm{\xi}_{h},\,\bm{u}\rangle_{L^{2}}\geq 0
, and so
\lambda_{u}\cdot\langle\bm{\xi}_{h},\,\bm{u}\rangle_{L^{2}}=0
.

The following claim is a key cog of the proof.

Claim 1
.

The following chains of implication hold:

(i)

We have
\|\mathsf{P}_{\bm{U}_{k}^{\mathrm{SE}}}^{\perp}{\widetilde{\bm{u}}}\|_{L^{2}}=0
if and only if
\|\mathsf{P}_{\bm{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{SE}}}^{\perp}{\widetilde{\bm{v}}}\|_{L^{2}}=0
, and if so then
\langle\bm{\xi}_{h},\,{\widetilde{\bm{u}}}\rangle_{L^{2}}=0
and
\langle\bm{\xi}_{g},\,{\widetilde{\bm{v}}}\rangle_{L^{2}}=0
.

(ii)

If
\|\mathsf{P}_{\bm{U}_{k}^{\mathrm{SE}}}^{\perp}{\widetilde{\bm{u}}}\|_{L^{2}}>0
and
\|\mathsf{P}_{\bm{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{SE}}}^{\perp}{\widetilde{\bm{v}}}\|_{L^{2}}>0
, then
\langle\bm{\xi}_{g},\,{\widetilde{\bm{v}}}\rangle_{L^{2}}>0
and
\langle\bm{\xi}_{h},\,{\widetilde{\bm{u}}}\rangle_{L^{2}}>0
.

We return to prove this claim shortly.
In view of the claim, there are two cases to consider.

Case 1:
\|\mathsf{P}_{\bm{U}_{k}^{\mathrm{SE}}}^{\perp}{\widetilde{\bm{u}}}\|_{L^{2}}=\|\mathsf{P}_{\bm{V}_{k}^{\mathrm{SE}}}^{\perp}{\widetilde{\bm{v}}}\|_{L^{2}}=\langle\bm{\xi}_{h},\,{\widetilde{\bm{u}}}\rangle_{L^{2}}=\langle\bm{\xi}_{g},\,{\widetilde{\bm{v}}}\rangle_{L^{2}}=0
:

Since we have adopted the convention that
\bm{0}/\|\bm{0}\|_{L^{2}}=0
, we may write

\displaystyle 0
